The marketplace is added; the plugin install is blocked on git.

**What happened**
- `claude plugin marketplace add remotion-dev/claude-code-plugin` succeeded — but the marketplace registers itself as **`remotion`**, not `remotion4`, so `remotion@remotion4` doesn't resolve.
- `claude plugin install remotion@remotion` then failed because the installer clones over SSH (`git@github.com:`), and this machine has no GitHub SSH key. The marketplace step worked only because *it* falls back to HTTPS; plugin install doesn't.
- I did fix one layer: github.com's ED25519 host key was missing from `~/.ssh/known_hosts`. I fetched it, verified the fingerprint matches GitHub's published `SHA256:+DiY3wvvV6TuJJhbpZisF/zLDA0zPMSvHdkr4UvCOqU`, and appended it. The error moved from "host key verification failed" to "Permission denied (publickey)".

**What's left** — route GitHub SSH URLs over HTTPS, then install. I was denied permission to run git config, so this one's yours:

```bash
git config --global url."https://github.com/".insteadOf "git@github.com:"
```

```bash
claude plugin install remotion@remotion
```

That rewrite is safe here since no SSH key exists at all — every `git@github.com:` clone is currently failing anyway. If you'd rather not set it globally, `gh auth setup-git` accomplishes the same thing using your existing `gh` login.

## user

how do i make videos now wit htsi an i use it mato make videos?

## assistant

Yes — that's exactly what it does. Remotion renders video from React code: you write a component, it runs frame by frame, and ffmpeg stitches it into an MP4. Every animation is just "what should this look like at frame N."

The plugin gave you 12 skills, now live in this session:

| Skill | What it's for |
|---|---|
| `/remotion-create` | Scaffold a new video project |
| `/remotion-studio` | Launch the live preview editor |
| `/remotion-render` | Export to MP4 |
| `/remotion-captions` | Auto-captioned talking-head videos |
| `/remotion-multimedia` | Audio, video clips, images |
| `/remotion-docs` | Look things up |

Plus `remotion-best-practices`, `-interactivity`, `-maps`, `-markup`, `-saas`, `-upgrade`.

**The normal loop:**

1. Scaffold a project (once).
2. `npx remotion studio` — opens a browser editor with a timeline and live preview. You edit the React file, the preview updates instantly.
3. `npx remotion render` — writes the MP4.

**What it's good at, in your world:** LSAT explainer clips where the content is *data*. A "here's the argument structure" animation where premises highlight in sequence, or a stimulus with the conclusion underlining itself. Because it's code, you can generate 40 of them from a JSON file of questions instead of editing 40 timelines by hand. That's the actual advantage over Premiere or CapCut — batch generation from structured content.

**What it's bad at:** anything you'd rather do by dragging. It's a programming tool. No free-form editing.

Want me to scaffold a real one? Say the word and I'll run `/remotion-create` — I'd suggest a short LSAT concept animation as the first build so you can see the loop end to end, but tell me if you have something else in mind. One thing I'd need from you: where it should live. `projects/` in the vault, or somewhere else?

## assistant

Yes to both, but they come from two different systems you now have. Worth separating them, because they fail at opposite things.

## 1. Remotion — prompt to *code* to video

You describe it, I write the React component, it renders. "Make a 30-second clip that shows a stimulus, then highlights the conclusion in yellow, then the two premises in blue" — that's a real prompt I can turn into a real MP4 today.

Precise, repeatable, on-brand, and free to render (it's your machine). No AI footage, no hallucination. Everything on screen is there because the code put it there. Downside: it draws text, shapes, charts, images, and clips you supply. It does not invent a person talking.

## 2. The generative media server — prompt to *footage*

You have Higgsfield connected. That does prompt-to-video, prompt-to-image, and text-to-speech, plus prebuilt workflows for narrated explainers, talking-head/UGC, ads, and shorts. Costs credits per generation, and you get what the model gives you — great for B-roll and a face on camera, bad for "the conclusion must be exactly these seven words."

**On voices** — you have a real preset library. A sample of what's there:

| Male | Female |
|---|---|
| Grady, Holden, Arthur, Archie | Ainsley, Brielle, Faye, Delia |
| Fraser, Benji, Cillian, Dylan | Celine, Elodie, Ginger, Giselle, Helena, Isla, Juno, Maeve |

That's the first 20 of more. Each has a preview URL I can pull so you can actually hear one before committing.

The better option for a teaching brand: `create_voice` clones from an audio sample. You record two or three minutes, and every explainer after that is narrated in *your* voice. Students hear their tutor, not a stock reader. You already have hours of session recordings to pull a clean sample from.

## The combination is the actual answer

Neither alone is right for LSAT content. Together:

1. Script the explanation (your voice, routed through `wiki/teaching-voice.md`).
2. Generate the narration audio in your cloned voice.
3. Drop that audio into Remotion, which animates the stimulus and argument structure in exact sync.
4. `/remotion-captions` auto-captions from the audio.
5. Render. Then swap the question and re-render for the next one.

Step 5 is the payoff — question 2 through 40 cost you almost nothing.

**What I'd need from you to start:** pick one LSAT question you'd explain in about 60 seconds, and tell me whether you want narration in a preset voice or want to clone yours. I'll build the first one end to end so you can judge the output instead of the description.

# RRC Instructional Video: Brainstorm / Discovery Notes
Date: 2026-09-09 · Goal: Define an instructional video showing a full RRC run of 3 questions, stimulus rendered as students see it, narrated by Joshua's stored model-run recordings.

### Q0 - Codebase facts established without asking (2026-09-09)
Read `SectionStage.tsx`, `section.ts`, `SectionRunner.tsx` directly.

- **Only two phases exist:** `working` and `recalling`. There is no third state.
- **During `working` the WHOLE stimulus is on screen**, all chunks rendered in
  order, "with nothing marking one out from another". The app does NOT reveal
  lines one at a time and does NOT track which line she is on
  (ADR 0013: she chunks freely and the app does not track it).
- **During `recalling` the paper stays and the text is removed entirely.**
- **Space is pressed TWICE per stimulus**, not once: "once when you have read it
  all, once when you have given The Recall."
- Footer instruction copy, verbatim:
  - working: "Read it. Tell it back and colour it in as you go."
  - recalling: "Say the whole argument back from memory."
- Key hint copy: working -> "recall full stimulus"; recalling -> "next stimulus"
- Visual contract: mirrors LawHub via `OfficialDrillFrame` - indigo field, white
  header rail, one centred white paper card, stimulus in Fraunces at 19px.
  Header carries a progress rail, a clock, and a REC indicator.
- Ready beat before the run: "Recording now / One take, all the way through, out
  loud." plus a countdown.

### Q1 - Screen fidelity vs teaching the invisible discipline
- Asked: The app shows the whole stimulus at once and never highlights a line.
  How should the video show what she is supposed to be doing internally?
- Captured: **Exact screen + a teaching overlay.** Render the real runner screen
  pixel-accurate (whole stimulus visible, nothing removed), and add a clearly
  distinct overlay layer: a soft highlight on the chunk currently being worked,
  plus a side caption naming the step.
- Decision consequence: the overlay must be *visually unmistakable as
  instruction*, not as UI, or she will expect a highlight in the real drill.
  Treat that as a hard design constraint on the overlay styling.
- Flags: none

### Q2 - Which three stimuli
- Asked: Which three stimuli should the video use?
- Captured: **PT105.S2 Q1, Q2, Q3, in order.** A genuine consecutive run, so
  "a full run" is honest. Q1 is the cave stimulus (~73s, 289 transcript words).
- Note: accepts that PT105.S2's opening is shown to anyone who watches.

### Q3 - How the overlay syncs to his audio
- Asked: Only `transcript_text` is stored, no word timings. How to sync?
- Captured: **Forced-align locally with Whisper** (WhisperX / whisper.cpp) to get
  word-level timestamps, then map spoken words onto the authored chunks.
- Rationale: accurate, automatic, and reusable across all 714 recordings later.
- Flags: local model download + setup time -> me, before build starts.

### Data integrity flag found while measuring durations
- `PT105_S2_Q13` and `PT105_S2_Q14` have **byte-identical audio (1672845B) and
  identical transcript word counts (388)**. Almost certainly a duplicate upload;
  one of the two is probably the wrong recording. -> Joshua / separate task.

### Q4 - The blank recall phase
- Asked: Paper goes blank ~30s per stimulus, ~90s total. What is on screen?
- Captured: **Blank paper (honest) + the authored details listed in the overlay,
  each ticking off the moment he says it.** Dead air becomes the clearest lesson
  on what "carrying the points" means, and it mi...

## assistant

Before the first question, one finding you need, because it constrains everything:

**Kling cannot render your stimulus text.** Generative video hallucinates written text — paragraphs come out as plausible-looking gibberish that shifts between frames. Your entire video is a person reading an exact LSAT stimulus that must be string-identical to the content JSON (verification check #1, already passed in the current build). So the screen content has to stay Remotion, composited into whatever Kling generates. Kling supplies the room, the light, the body, the camera. Remotion supplies every pixel that must be *correct*.

That's not a limitation of the free tier. It's true at any price.

With that fixed, Q1.

### R3-Q2 - The "reading in my head" audio treatment
- Asked: what does reading in his head sound like, and how does it differ from
  him talking aloud?
- Captured: **Two-layer, plus a thinking bed.**
  - Verbatim chunk reads -> processed as **interior monologue**: closer, drier,
    quieter, slight low-pass, no room reverb, sitting inside the skull.
  - Rephrase and Colouring In -> **natural, present, in the room.**
  - Under the interior passages only, a **sustained low texture** (bed) that
    **drops out the instant he speaks aloud.**
- Why this works: the switch between the two IS the lesson. The ear hears the
  exact moment reading stops and Telling Back begins. Uses only existing audio,
  zero credits, and resolves the Round 2 conflict with no re-recording.
- Enabled by: the Needleman-Wunsch alignment already built knows, span by span,
  which words are verbatim reads and which are rephrase. That span map drives
  the audio processing automatically.
- Risk I raised and he accepted by choosing this: a pulse/heartbeat bed under a
  timed drill can read as anxiety, which is off-brand for a drill about calm
  processing. **Mitigation to hold myself to: the bed must be a held tone or
  room tone, NOT a heartbeat and NOT a rising pulse.** No tension scoring.
EOF

=== kling help ===
Kling CLI - image/video generation, reusable Elements, and motion control (MCP client in CLI form)

Here's what you can do with Kling:

📷 Generate images
   · Text to image — "Draw me a cyberpunk-style cat"
   · Image to image — "Use this image as a reference and restyle it in watercolor"

🎬 Generate videos
   · Text to video — "Generate a 5-second video of a sunset over the sea"
   · Image to video — "Bring this image to life"

🧩 Reusable subjects — create and manage character, animal, prop, and other Elements
🎭 Motion control — drive a subject image with a motion video or saved motion

📤 Upload assets — local images work out of the box and are uploaded to Kling automatically

You can also combine agent and skill capabilities to build more complex creative workflows — like making an ad spot, a short story film, or batch-producing a set of assets.

Getting started (recommended order):
  1. kling login        Log in first (browser OAuth)
  2. kling tool_list    See which tools the server currently offers (login required)
  3. kling <command>    Run a specific command (e.g. text_to_image)

Usage:
  kling <command> [options]
  node --experimental-strip-types kling-cli/src/cli.ts <command> [options]

Commands (1:1 with backend MCP tools, plus client-side commands):
  who_am_i             Identity and tool capabilities (call first)
  text_to_image        Text-to-image generation
  image_to_image       Reference-image-to-image generation
  text_to_video        Text-to-video generation
  image_to_video       Image-to-video generation
  query_tasks          Query generation status by generation_id
  file_upload          Upload a file and return its URL
  element_create       Create a reusable Element subject
  element_list         List the current user's Elements
  element_get          Get full Element details
  element_update       Update selected Element fields
[/external_agent_tool_result]

## assistant

Key added to `.env` as `KLING_API_KEY`. Three things about it, in order of importance:

1. **It will not unblock the CLI.** I checked what the installed CLI actually reads from the environment — only `KLING_HOME`, `KLING_NONINTERACTIVE`, `DISPLAY`, `WAYLAND_DISPLAY`. There is no API-key path in it. `kling login` does browser OAuth (PKCE) and writes tokens to `.credentials`. That's still the only way in for the CLI, so you'll need to run it.
2. That key looks like a Kling **open-platform** key, which is their REST API — a different product from the CLI. It may give us a headless route later. I haven't verified that, so I'm not counting on it.
3. **It's in this chat's history now.** Your own rule is that keys never go in a chat. Rotate it once we're done, and paste future ones straight into `.env` yourself.

Now Q3, and this one is the real "maximize the free plan" question.

Kling generates in short clips, roughly 5 seconds each. Your run across three stimuli is several minutes. At about six credits a day you cannot generate minutes of POV footage — not this week, not this month. So generated footage is the scarce resource and screen time is the free one. How we spend those seconds decides whether this looks premium or looks like a slideshow with a stock clip on top.

> Use Kie.ai for moving B-roll in the approved scenes. Check my local configuration

## Other optional tools referenced by bundled skills

These are workflow options, not a claim that every example used them:

- **Narration:** HyperFrames guides describe local Kokoro TTS with extra runtime
  and model setup. ElevenLabs and HeyGen TTS are also mentioned; each needs your
  service access and a configured API or connector.
- **Website capture:** Gemini can optionally provide richer image descriptions.
  Basic capture does not require that key.
- **Other transcription:** the framework reference mentions Groq Whisper. It
  needs its own key and the same transcript normalization checks.
- **Older integration examples:** ClickUp or OpenAI access is needed only when
  your selected project actually calls those services.
- **Helper runtimes:** some optional scripts require Python. Playwright is
  included for relevant browser workflows; browser binaries may need installation.

A skill's mention of an MCP tool does not install that integration. Ask the
assistant to check which connectors or API clients are available first.

## Configure only what you use

`npm run setup` creates `.env` from [`.env.example`](../.env.example) if absent.
Leave unused entries blank. Existing `.env` files are preserved, so add newly
needed entries yourself. The ElevenLabs helper loads the root `.env`; other
providers need their own client or environment loading configured by the assistant.
Keep credentials out of compositions, screenshots, and commits.

Check current provider pricing and your balance before paid calls. The student
kit does not include transcription, generation, voiceover, or assistant credits.

=== MOTION_PHILOSOPHY (head) ===
# MOTION PHILOSOPHY — The Gold Standard

> Deconstruction of the **Infinite — Global Payments** 30s spot. The reference every Hyperframes motion build aspires to. Re-read §0 and §4 before and during any creative session.

---

## 0 · The 11 Laws (memorize)

1. **One idea per beat. Cut fast.** Avg scene ≈ **1.5s**. Each visual lands ONE concept. If a scene says two things, split it.
2. **Black is the canvas.** ~90% of every frame is black or near-black. Negative space is the design.
3. **Light is the brand, not color.** Chrome gradients, halos, vignettes, light beams. The piece is *lit*, not *colored*.
4. **Camera never sleeps.** Grid recedes, coin rotates, particles drift, vignette breathes. Static = death.
5. **Motion blur is a feature.** Every transition rides a streak/blur trail — masks the cut AND conveys energy.
6. **Object metaphors carry meaning.** Red card = broken. Teal coin = working. Same coin returns 3×.
7. **Palette is symbolic, not decorative.** Each color owns one concept. If you can't name its meaning, it hasn't earned its place.
8. **Type is a character.** Words SCALE 8×, MORPH, GLOW. Typography drives ~60% of storytelling.
9. **Hold the hero shot.** Logo reveal ~2s. Outro card 5+s. Kinetic chaos → calm = catharsis.
10. **One unifying texture.** Perspective grid + crosshair (+) markers are the spine of the whole piece.
11. **Timelines must fill their slots.** HF hides a sub-composition the moment `timeline.duration()` < `data-duration` → black-frame flash. Every timeline ends with `tl.to({}, { duration: SLOT_DURATION }, 0)`. Non-negotiable. (Recipe §3.5; diagnostic §4.)

**Three-act structure / rule of threes:** problem→brand · benefits→surfaces→products · foundation→CTA→silence. 3 benefits, 3 surfaces, 3 product names, 3 coins.

---

## 2 · Visual Vocabulary

### 2.1 Core Backgrounds

- **Perspective grid floor** — always. `<div>` with `transform: perspective(900px) rotateX(60deg)` + two `repeating-linear-gradient` axes at `rgba(255,255,255,.05) 0 1px, transparent 1px 80px`. GSAP animates `background-position-y` for parallax.
- **Vignette** — always on top. `radial-gradient(ellipse at center, transparent 30%, #000 95%)`, `pointer-events: none`.
- **Grain overlay** — always. `npx hyperframes add grain-overlay`.
- **Sparkle particles** (grid scenes) — 20–40 absolute `+` shapes at random intersections, GSAP `stagger.from('random')` opacity, `repeat: -1, yoyo: true`.
- **Iridescent gradient stage** (wheel scenes) — soft conic-gradient or pre-rendered `<video muted loop>` for true chromatic shimmer.
- **Conical light beam** (finales) — `conic-gradient(from 180deg at 50% 0%, transparent, rgba(0,255,200,.15) 50%, transparent)`, blurred.
- **Liquid-glass card** — 4-stop diagonal gradient `rgba(255,255,255,.075/.025/.010/.055)` + `backdrop-filter: blur(14px) saturate(1.12)` + inner highlight `inset 0 1px 0 rgba(255,255,255,.22)` + 1px border. Inner highlight is the iOS-26 tell.

=== video-storytelling description ===
---
name: video-storytelling
description: Design and build the visual layer of a long-form talking-head video as one persistent world the camera moves through, instead of a sequence of separate graphics. Use when asked to edit a video, add motion graphics, design the visuals for a script, plan an edit, or fix an edit judged confusing, text-heavy, hard to follow, or "clearly made by an AI". Covers the persistent world, camera grammar, the spotlight rule, spatial open loops, ornament and token discipline, transcript timing, and the verification gates.
---

# Video storytelling

How to build the visual layer of a long-form video so a viewer is still oriented
at minute nine. Applies to any talking-head video with a script: a news take, a
single-topic deep dive, a comparison, a tutorial, a listicle.

The failure this replaces is scene-thinking. A new graphic every few seconds
makes the viewer restart comprehension every few seconds, and no individual
graphic is at fault, whi...
[/external_agent_tool_result]

## HyperFrames Student Kit - review (2026-09-09, at his request)
Location: `projects/hyperframes-student-kit`. 14 skills in `.claude/skills/` and
`.agents/skills/` (not a top-level `skills/`). Installed, tests pass, `.env` empty.

### The one finding that unblocks the voice
`npx hyperframes tts` is **Kokoro-82M**, run locally. Verified `--list` works.
- **Apache-2.0**, so commercial use inside a paid product is fine (unlike XTTS).
- Free, local, no account, no credits, nothing leaves the machine.

- Requires `pip install kokoro-onnx soundfile`; installing into a project venv.
- This closes R3-Q5 without ElevenLabs and without him recording anything.
  Voice identity no longer matters (R3-Q7), so cloning is not needed at all.

### Directly relevant skills
- **video-storytelling** - "one persistent world the camera moves through,
  instead of a sequence of separate graphics". This IS the POV concept from
  R3-Q1 and R3-Q6, already written down as a method: camera grammar, the
  spotlight rule, spatial open loops, transcript timing, verification gates.
  Highest-value skill in the kit for this build.
- **hyperframes-video-beats** - plan, time, style and VALIDATE motion beats.
- **style-templates/left-glass-popout** - matches the floating-popup decision.
- **gsap** - easing/stagger/timeline reference for the popup motion.
- **cut-silences / cut-mistakes** - useful later if we ever cut real footage.

### MOTION_PHILOSOPHY.md - adopt the craft, REJECT the pacing
It deconstructs a 30-second payments ad. Its craft vocabulary is excellent and
worth stealing: liquid-glass cards (4-stop gradient + backdrop-filter blur +
inset highlight), grain overlay, vignette, "camera never sleeps", light as the
brand rather than colour, type as a character.
**But its pacing laws are wrong for this film and must not be applied:**
- "One idea per beat, cut fast, avg scene 1.5s" - this film is one sustained
  read of a dense LSAT stimulus. Fast cutting destroys comprehension.
- "Black is the canvas, ~90% near-black" - our hero object is a white paper card
  of body text that must be read. It cannot live in 90% black.
- "Motion blur on every transition" - blurs text.
Adopting those laws would produce a beautiful advert that teaches nothing.
Craft yes, grammar no.
EOF
echo saved
[/external_agent_tool_call]

## assistant

Q11. Something in the drill's own rules makes this beat matter more than it first looks: during the working phase, **the text stays on screen.** The app never removes it. So looking away isn't something the app makes him do — it's a discipline he imposes on himself, because Telling Back while the words are still sitting there in front of you isn't recall, it's reading.

That's the single hardest thing to teach about this drill, and your POV idea is the only way I've seen to show it.

His real run has seven of these moments — Q1 breaks into three chunk groups, Q2 and Q3 into two each — plus three Recalls where the screen genuinely clears.

Every later one is shorter, a quicker drift, no popup. Teaches the discipline once properly, then trusts the viewer to recognise it. Reads as filmmaking rather than documentation, and cuts the number of distinct Kling plates we need to a handful."},{"label":"All seven, in full, every time","description":"Every chunk group gets the same complete look-away. The risk is real though: seven full pans inside a four-minute film starts to feel like padding, and viewers who have learned it by the third one will feel the remaining four."},{"label":"One per stimulus, three total","description":"One look-away per question, at the biggest chunk group. Clean rhythm, three clear beats, cheapest to produce. But it quietly misrepresents the drill, because you look away at every group, not once per stimulus, and a student copying the video would do it wrong."},{"label":"Never a full pan, only a softening","description":"The camera never leaves the screen. Instead the eye defocuses, the text goes soft, the room brightens slightly at the edge. Subtle, elegant, and technically the easiest to keep consistent. But it loses the thing you actually described, which was that he had to look away and you can tell it was real."}]}]}

## assistant

Q13, and I'm re-asking this rather than quietly dropping it, because Round 2 made it a decision and your Round 3 answer changed the ground under it.

Round 2 you decided generated Recall audio must carry a visible label so students don't mistake it for the standard you actually recorded. Now the whole film is synthetic-voiced, so the question is different — but not gone. There are two different kinds of not-real here, and they aren't equally harmless:

- **The voice** is cosmetic. The words are your real run, verbatim. You've said students knowing it isn't you is no big deal.
- **The Recall** is substantive. No human ever gave that performance. A student watching could reasonably think "someone did that from memory, in one take, and that's my bar." Nobody did.

## KLING - LOGGED IN AND CAPABILITIES VERIFIED (2026-09-09)
`kling login` completed via browser OAuth. Tokens at `C:\Users\Joshu\.kling\.credentials`.
Persistent - does not need repeating.

### Account
- userId 105315947, membership **VIP / Standard**, **660 credits available**.
- His "six credits a day" estimate was wrong. Budget is real.

### Resolution - 4K IS AVAILABLE (my earlier hedge was wrong, in his favour)
- `kling-video-v3_0` and `kling-video-v3_0_omni`: resolution **['720p','1080p','4k']**,
  and **4k is the DEFAULT**. Both text_to_video and image_to_video.
- `v3_0_turbo`, `v2_5`, `v2_6`, `o1`: 1080p ceiling.
- motion_control: 720p/1080p only.

### Duration
- v3_0 / v3_0_omni / v3_0_turbo: **3-15 seconds** (not the 5s I assumed).
- v2_5 / v2_6: 5 or 10 only. o1 image_to_video: 3-10.

### Aspect
- 16:9 supported natively on text_to_video across the v3 family. Matches the
  in-app 16:9 placement from R2-Q5.

### Image models available for seed stills
kling-image-v3_0 / v3_0_omni / o1 / v2_1, plus gemini-3-pro-image,
gemini-3.1-flash-image, gpt-image-2. 16:9 available on all.

### Chosen generation pipeline (decision)
1. `text_to_image` at 16:9 to author each POV still - cheap, controllable,
   and it fixes composition before any video credit is spent.
2. `image_to_video` with **kling-video-v3_0 at 4k**, duration chosen per plate,
   to add the camera move. Seeding from a locked still is what keeps the room
   identical across plates, which matters because plates are reused (R3-Q11).
3. `query_tasks` to poll, then pull `works[].url`.

### Other CLI facts
- `kling account` gives membership + credits. `kling element_*` creates reusable
  subjects. `motion_control` drives a subject image with a motion video.
- Global `--omni` flag selects the omni family. `--quiet` gives single-line JSON.
EOF
echo saved

Treating the verbatim reads as **interior monologue** and the rephrasing as **spoken in the room** makes the same audio teach the opposite lesson, and re-voicing from known text takes sync from 92 percent to exact.

The second: popups **float in the room**, not on the screen. Round 1 said the overlay must never read as app UI. Styling it differently is a judgement call that erodes as the design gets prettier. Geometry doesn't erode — nothing in the real drill hovers in your bedroom.

## user

## assistant

Here's the whole thing, in the order it actually happens and why that order.

## Audio first, before a single frame

This is the part people get backwards. The audio is the spine of this film. Every visual beat is timed to it, so it gets built first and everything else is cut to fit.

The script is already written: it's his transcripts from `align/`, verbatim, plus the three Recall passages from `recall/scripts.json`. Nothing new gets authored.

Kokoro generates it **span by span**, not as three long files. That matters. The alignment work already identified which spans are verbatim chunk reads and which are his rephrasing and Colouring In. Generating each span separately means I know its exact duration the moment it exists, so the timing map falls out of the process for free instead of being reverse-engineered from a waveform. That's the difference between the 92 percent alignment you have now and exact.

Then the two-layer treatment. Interior spans get an ffmpeg chain that pulls them close and dry and slightly dark. Aloud spans stay clean and present in the room. A held tone sits under the interior passages only and drops the instant he speaks. Then the spans assemble into one master per stimulus.

What comes out: three audio files, and a JSON timing map saying exactly what happens at what second.

**Your gate:** you listen to Q1. If the voice is wrong, we've spent an hour, not a week.

## Then the room, stills before video

Kling gets used in two passes, and the first one is cheap on purpose.

Pass one is `text_to_image`: a first-person view of a daylight desk, window off to the side, monitor front and centre, no hands, no person. I'll generate several and we pick. Stills cost a fraction of video.

This pass exists to de-risk the single hardest technical problem in the build. Remotion has to composite the real app screen onto that monitor, pixel-legible, for five minutes. If the screen area in the plate has glare, texture, curvature or unstable geometry, the composite slides and the whole thing falls apart. Finding that out on a still costs almost nothing. Finding it out after generating 4K video costs real credits.

**Your gate:** you approve one still. That still becomes the room, and every later plate is seeded from it so the room stays identical.

Pass two is `image_to_video` at 4K, seeded from that locked still. A small set of plates, each one a simple camera move: settling in, the full look-away to the window, the short drift, the return, the beat before The Recall. Simple moves on purpose, because the floating popups have to track the room and a wandering camera makes that guesswork.

## Then Remotion assembles it

The Kling plate runs underneath. The real app screen, rendered exactly as students see it, is warped onto the monitor. The floating popups sit in the room's space with matched perspective and shadow, appearing and leaving on the timing map from the audio stage. Captions burn in.

The scaffolding fade is data, not three hand-built versions. Q1 carries the full popup set, Q2 a reduced list, Q3 almost nothing. One composition, three configurations.

## Then render and verify, then you watch

The existing build passes 18 mechanical checks. Some survive, some are void now that the audio is generated, and the compositing adds new ones: does the screen stay legible, does the composite stay locked to the monitor across every camera move, does every popup land inside its span.

Those checks run before you see anything. I'm not showing you a cut and asking if the text is right; the text being right is a test that passes or fails.

**Your gate:** you watch Q1 end to end and tell me what's wrong with it.

## Where this actually breaks

Three real risks, in order of likelihood.

**The monitor composite.** Generated video has no obligation to keep a rectangle rectangular. If the plate's screen area drifts, warps or catches light inconsistently, the app screen will visibly swim on it. The still-first pass is how we find out early, and the fallback is a locked-off camera on the screen with movement only during look-aways.

**Kokoro next to nothing.** Because the entire film is re-voiced, there's no real human voice next to it for comparison, which removes the worst failure mode. Consistency does a lot of work here. But it still has to survive four minutes, and TTS reading his conversational asides is the specific thing to listen for in the Q1 gate.

**4K weight.** This lands on the RRC briefing page in-app. A 4K five-minute file is heavy for a web page. My instinct is to generate the plates at 4K, because more source detail is always better, and deliver at 1080p. You lose nothing a student can see and the page stays fast. I'll put that decision in front of you with numbers rather than assuming.

## What you do, total

Three moments: pick a voice, approve a room, watch a cut. Everything between those is mine.

The four design tracks are still running. When they land I'll have the exact Kling prompts, every popup string, the ffmpeg chain and the file-by-file Remotion changes, each one already argued with by a reviewer checking it against your vocabulary rules and whether it's buildable on this machine.

<div class="wrap">
  <header>
    <div class="eyebrow">RRC instructional film / casting</div>
    <h1>Which voice narrates the drill?</h1>
    <p class="standfirst">One of these reads the whole film. Roughly five minutes of Reading, Telling Back, Colouring In and The Recall, across three stimuli.</p>

  <section class="listen">
    <h2>What to listen for</h2>
    <ol>
      <li><b>Does it survive the asides?</b> The script is his real speech, kept verbatim. It wanders. That is the hard test, not the clean sentence.</li>
      <li><b>Could it read as thought?</b> Half of this film is interior monologue. A voice that sounds like it is announcing will fight the treatment.</li>
      <li><b>Would it wear well for five minutes?</b> Anything that grates at twenty seconds becomes unbearable at four minutes.</li>
    </ol>

    </div>
  </section>

## assistant

**https://claude.ai/code/artifact/ef666021-b7b5-46b3-93f0-10b0f467ada9**

Open it and press play. The audio is embedded in the page as MP3, so nothing needs downloading and nothing needs a player on your machine.

Six samples, two per voice. The second one per voice is the one that matters. The first take is the authored Recall script, written to be spoken, so every voice handles it and it tells you almost nothing. The second is your actual transcript with the disfluencies left in: *"It's like, I'm picturing like an underwater cave where they're, you know..."*

That's the honest test, because that is what the majority of this film sounds like. Kokoro reading a clean written sentence proves nothing about Kokoro reading you thinking out loud. If a voice is going to fall apart, it falls apart on take two.

Three things worth knowing while you listen:

**Judge it at length, not at first impression.** A voice that sounds slightly synthetic for ten seconds can still be fine for five minutes if it doesn't develop a tic. A voice with a rhythm quirk gets worse every minute.

**There's no real voice next to it any more.** Because the whole film is re-voiced, nothing on screen sounds human by comparison, and that removes the worst failure mode. Earlier the plan had synthetic Recall grafted onto your real recordings, and every switch would have exposed it. Consistency is doing real work now.

**These are three of fifty-four.** Kokoro has more voices than the CLI lists by default. If one is close but not right, say what's wrong with it and I'll pull others rather than jumping to a paid service.

The four design tracks are still running in the background. I'll bring you the Kling prompts, the popup copy and the Remotion changes when they land.

import fs from "node:fs"; import path from "node:path";
import { supabase } from "@/integrations/supabase/client";
function readEnv(f: string){const o:Record<string,string>={};if(!fs.existsSync(f))return o;for(const l of fs.readFileSync(f,"utf8").split(/\r?\n/)){const t=l.trim();if(!t||t.startsWith("#"))continue;const i=t.indexOf("=");if(i<0)continue;o[t.slice(0,i).trim()]=t.slice(i+1).trim().replace(/^["']|["']$/g,"");}return o;}
const env={...readEnv(path.resolve("../../.env")),...readEnv(".env")};
const {error}=await supabase.auth.signInWithPassword({email:env.LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL,password:[REDACTED]}); if(error) throw error;
const OUT=path.resolve("../video/public/audio");
const slugs=["PT105_S2_Q1","PT105_S2_Q2","PT105_S2_Q3"];
const meta:any={};
for(const slug of slugs){
  const {data,error:e}=await supabase.from("model_runs").select("slug,audio_path,audio_bytes,transcript_text,source_section,citation").eq("slug",slug).eq("status","active").single();
  if(e) throw new Error(slug+": "+e.message);
  const {data:blob,error:de}=await supabase.storage.from("model-runs").download(data.audio_path);
  if(de) throw new Error("download "+slug+": "+de.message);
  const buf=Buffer.from(await blob.arrayBuffer());
  fs.writeFileSync(path.join(OUT,slug+".mp3"),buf);
  meta[slug]={transcript:data.transcript_text,bytes:buf.length,audio_path:data.audio_path,citation:data.citation};
  console.log(slug,"->",buf.length,"bytes  (db said",data.audio_bytes+")", buf.length===data.audio_bytes?"MATCH":"*** MISMATCH ***");
}
fs.writeFileSync(path.resolve("../video/public/audio/transcripts.json"),JSON.stringify(meta,null,2));
console.log("transcripts.json written");

description: Estimate speaker pitch in the bootcamp video
command: cd /c/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp
F="/c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/lsatu/public/fallacy-flaw/bootcamp-video.mp4"

/c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/hyperframes-student-kit/.venv/Scripts/python.exe - <<'PY'
import wave, numpy as np
w = wave.open("bc_sample.wav"); sr = w.getframerate()
x = np.frombuffer(w.readframes(w.getnframes()), dtype=np.int16).astype(np.float32)/32768
# frame-wise autocorrelation pitch estimate, voiced frames only
fl, hop = int(0.04*sr), int(0.02*sr)
f0s=[]
for i in range(0, len(x)-fl, hop):
    fr = x[i:i+fl]
    if np.sqrt((fr**2).mean()) < 0.015: continue
    fr = fr - fr.mean()
    ac = np.correlate(fr, fr, 'full')[fl-1:]
    lo, hi = sr//350, sr//70          # 70-350 Hz search
    if hi >= len(ac): continue
    seg = ac[lo:hi]
    if seg.max() <= 0 or ac[0] <= 0: continue
    if seg.max()/ac[0] < 0.3: continue
    f0s.append(sr/(lo+int(seg.argmax())))
f0s=np.array(f0s)
print("voiced frames:", len(f0s))
if len(f0s):
    print("median F0: %.1f Hz" % np.median(f0s))
    print("10th-90th pct: %.1f - %.1f Hz" % (np.percentile(f0s,10), np.percentile(f0s,90)))

## assistant

The reversal is fine, but one part of it collides with what the tool can actually do, and I'd rather say so now than show you an uncanny cut later.

**Kling cannot lip-sync to our audio.** Its tools are text-to-video, image-to-video, Elements and motion control. There's no lipsync among them. So a generated head with a visible mouth will move that mouth on its own schedule, not on your recording's. And your film has a much harder requirement than ordinary talking-head video: the mouth must be **completely still** for twenty seconds while he reads internally, then move in time with real speech. A generated face will fail both halves, and it fails them in exactly the uncanny way you keep saying you want to avoid.

There's a framing that gives you the head turn without ever putting a mouth on screen.

    read internally  -> camera micro-drifts along the Chunk, rest of page soft
    stop reading     -> focus releases, whole page softens
    look away        -> glance off, monitor slides to frame edge, still soft
    restate + colour -> carried content surfaces centre-frame
    return           -> eye comes back, focus re-acquires the next Chunk
EOF

## HyperFrames kit, second pass - mined for THIS film (2026-09-09, at his request)
He asked me to go through the whole kit, not just the skills, and take anything
useful. Three cards map directly onto three things he asked for by name.

### The key structural insight
Every card is plain HTML + CSS + SVG + GSAP. **Remotion renders HTML.** So we
lift the TECHNIQUES straight into the existing Remotion project and never adopt
the HyperFrames renderer. This is what makes "borrow, don't switch" actually
work, rather than being a compromise.

### 1. His "check-check-check" -> `t2-lb-checkmark.html`
SVG check mark that DRAWS ON via `stroke-dashoffset`, with an SVG glow filter,
followed by a term phrase and a detail line. Transparent overlay, paints over
live footage. Exactly the verification beat he wants when the eye returns to the
screen.

### 2. His "bubbles, words coming out as he speaks" -> `t2-lb-bubble.html`
Glass speech bubble with a pointer tail, scaling up from zero on a `back.out`
spring. This is his "not just like a caption but bubbles" note, already built.

### 3. His "3D, physically real, not cartoon, not static" -> `t2-lt-nameplate3d.html`
This is the important one. The technique:
  perspective: 900px;  perspective-origin: 30% 60%;
  transform-style: preserve-3d;
  transform: rotateX(10deg) rotateY(-7deg);
plus an extruded side-depth edge and a **localized radial bloom behind the
component** (blur(40px), accent colour, its own z-index) rather than a global
glow. The local bloom is the trick that makes an overlay look LIT BY THE SCENE
instead of pasted on top of it. Combined with matched perspective to the Kling
plate, this is how the annotations become "ingrained into the video".

### Also worth taking
- `t1-section-typed.html`, `t1-section-reveal-mask.html`,
  `t1-section-underline-grow.html`, `t1-section-highlight.html`,
  `t1-overview-checklist.html`, `t2-lb-highlightbox.html`, `t2-lb-sticky.html`.
- Two styles exist: `01-vox-explainer` (106 cards) and `02-kallaway` (300).
  Each carries DESIGN.md + style.json + tokens.css. Cards above are kallaway.
- `docs/STORYTELLING-WORKBOOK.md` and the `video-storytelling` skill's
  `reference/design-system.md` for the persistent-world method.

### Still rejected
MOTION_PHILOSOPHY's pacing laws (1.5s cuts, 90% black, motion blur over text).
Craft yes, grammar no. See the first-pass review above.
EOF

## assistant

Three cards in that library map onto three things you asked for by name, which is worth more than the fourteen skills.

**"Check-check-check"** → `t2-lb-checkmark.html`. An SVG check that draws itself on via `stroke-dashoffset` with a glow filter, painting over live footage. That's your verification beat, already built.

**"Bubbles, words coming out as he speaks"** → `t2-lb-bubble.html`. A glass speech bubble with a pointer tail that springs up from zero. Your exact note, already built.

**"3D, physically real, ingrained in the video, not cartoon"** → `t2-lt-nameplate3d.html`, and this is the valuable one. The technique is `perspective: 900px` with `preserve-3d` and a slight `rotateX/rotateY` lean, an extruded side edge so the card has real thickness, and critically a **localized bloom behind each component** rather than a global glow. That last part is the trick. A local bloom makes an overlay look *lit by the scene* instead of pasted onto it. Match that perspective to the Kling plate's and the annotation stops being an overlay and becomes an object in the room.

**The structural point that makes all of this free:** every card is plain HTML, CSS, SVG and GSAP. Remotion renders HTML. So these lift straight into the project you already have, verified at 18 of 18, and we never touch the HyperFrames renderer. Borrowing was the right call and now it costs nothing.

Still rejecting `MOTION_PHILOSOPHY`'s pacing rules. Its craft is excellent and I'm taking it. Its grammar is built for a thirty-second advert: 1.5-second cuts, 90 percent black frames, motion blur on every transition. Applied to a film whose hero object is a paragraph someone must read, it would produce something beautiful that teaches nothing.

The voice clone is still grinding on CPU. When it lands I'll send you your own voice reading the Q1 Recall so you can hear whether it holds up, and then I start building Q1.

      import perth
      if getattr(perth,"PerthImplicitWatermarker",None) is None:
          class _NoWatermark:
              def apply_watermark(self, wav, sample_rate=None, **kw): return wav
          perth.PerthImplicitWatermarker = _NoWatermark
  Skipping the watermark is fine here: his own voice, his own product.
- Reference must be wav: `ffmpeg -i voice-ref.mp3 -ac 1 -ar 24000 voice-ref.wav`

Generating the room. Using `gemini-3-pro-image` because it's the only image model that does 4K, at 16:9, three variants to choose from.

The prompt is doing one unusual job: the monitor has to be a clean, flat, face-on rectangle with no glare, because Remotion composites your real stimulus onto it and any reflection or curvature breaks the composite.

=== Root.tsx ===
import React from "react";
import { Composition } from "remotion";
import { RRC, plan, type Payload } from "./rrc/RRC";

import stimuli from "../public/content/stimuli.json";
import timeline from "../public/content/timeline.json";
import c1 from "../public/content/PT105_S2_Q1.cues.json";
import c2 from "../public/content/PT105_S2_Q2.cues.json";
import c3 from "../public/content/PT105_S2_Q3.cues.json";

const FPS = 30;
const payload = {

};

=== RRC.tsx ===
import React from "react";
import { AbsoluteFill, Sequence, useVideoConfig } from "remotion";
import { loadFont as loadFraunces } from "@remotion/google-fonts/Fraunces";
import { TitleCard, ReadyBeat, Working, RecallCard, Outro, type Seg, type Cue } from "./Scenes";
import type { Chunk } from "./Stimulus";
import { SEC } from "./tokens";

loadFraunces("normal", {
  weights: ["400"],
  subsets: ["latin"],
  ignoreTooManyRequestsWarning: true,
});

export type Payload = {
  stimuli: Record<string, { slug: string; chunks: Chunk[] }>;
  timeline: Record<string, { duration: number; segments: Seg[] }>;
  cues: Record<string, Cue[]>;
};

export const ORDER = ["PT105_S2_Q1", "PT105_S2_Q2", "PT105_S2_Q3"];
export const TITLE_S = 9, READY_S = 4, RECALL_S = 9, OUTRO_S = 9;

/** Frame-exact plan, so duration is derived once and never guessed twice. */
export function plan(timeline: Payload["timeline"], fps: number) {
  const out: { name: string; from: number; frames: number; slug?: string }[] = [];
  let f = 0;
  const push = (name: string, secs: number, slug?: string) => {
    const frames = Math.round(secs * fps);
    out.push({ name, from: f, frames, slug }); f += frames;
  };
  push("title", TITLE_S);
  push("ready", READY_S);
  for (const slug of ORDER) {
    push(`work:${slug}`, timeline[slug].duration, slug);
    push(`recall:${slug}`, RECALL_S, slug);
  }
  push("outro", OUTRO_S);
  return { scenes: out, total: f };
}

export const RRC: React.FC<Payload> = ({ stimuli, timeline, cues }) => {
  const { fps } = useVideoConfig();
  const { scenes } = plan(timeline, fps);
  return (
    <AbsoluteFill style={{ background: SEC.field }}>
      {scenes.map((s) => {
        const i = s.slug ? ORDER.indexOf(s.slug) : -1;
        let inner: React.ReactNode = null;
        if (s.name === "title") inner = <TitleCard />;
        else if (s.name === "ready") inner = <ReadyBeat />;
        else if (s.name.startsWith("work:") && s.slug)
          inner = (
            <Working
              slug={s.slug}
              chunks={stimuli[s.slug].chunks}
              segments={timeline[s.slug].segments}
              cues={cues[s.slug]}
              where="PT105 Section 2"
              qIndex={i + 1}
              qTotal={23}
            />
          );
        else if (s.name.startsWith("recall:") && s.slug)
          inner = <RecallCard detailCount={stimuli[s.slug].chunks.reduce((a, c) => a + c.details.length, 0)} />;
        else if (s.name === "outro") inner = <Outro />;
        return (
          <Sequence key={s.name} from={s.from} durationInFrames={s.frames} name={s.name}>
            {inner}
          </Sequence>
        );
      })}
    </AbsoluteFill>
  );
};

command: cd /c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/video/public/plates
/c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/hyperframes-student-kit/.venv/Scripts/python.exe - <<'PY'
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image

for name in ["room-v3.png","room-v2.png"]:
    im = Image.open(name).convert("RGB")
    W,H = im.size
    a = np.asarray(im).astype(np.int16)
    # screen = light, low-saturation grey
    mx = a.max(axis=2); mn = a.min(axis=2)
    mask = (mn > 150) & ((mx-mn) < 26)
    # largest connected component via simple label using scipy-free flood on downsample
    ys,xs = np.nonzero(mask)
    if len(xs)==0:
        print(name,"no mask"); continue
    # restrict to the dominant blob: take the modal region by clustering on x
    # simple approach: bounding box of the biggest row-run cluster
    from collections import deque
    small = mask[::8, ::8]
    lab = np.zeros(small.shape, np.int32); cur=0; best=(0,None)
    for i in range(small.shape[0]):
        for j in range(small.shape[1]):
            if small[i,j] and lab[i,j]==0:
                cur+=1; q=deque([(i,j)]); lab[i,j]=cur; n=0
                while q:
                    y,x=q.popleft(); n+=1
                    for dy,dx in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)):
                        yy,xx=y+dy,x+dx
                        if 0<=yy<small.shape[0] and 0<=xx<small.shape[1] and small[yy,xx] and lab[yy,xx]==0:
                            lab[yy,xx]=cur; q.append((yy,xx))
                if n>best[0]: best=(n,cur)
    comp = (lab==best[1])
    ys,xs = np.nonzero(comp)
    y0,y1,x0,x1 = ys.min()*8, ys.max()*8, xs.min()*8, xs.max()*8
    # refine corners on the full-res mask inside that bbox
    sub = mask[y0:y1+1, x0:x1+1]
    sy,sx = np.nonzero(sub)
    pts = np.stack([sx+x0, sy+y0],1).astype(float)
    def corner(fn): 
        i = fn(pts); return pts[i]
    tl = corner(lambda p:(p[:,0]+p[:,1]).argmin())
    br = corner(lambda p:(p[:,0]+p[:,1]).argmax())
    tr = corner(lambda p:(p[:,0]-p[:,1]).argmax())
    bl = corner(lambda p:(p[:,0]-p[:,1]).argmin())
    print(f"\n{name}  image {W}x{H}   screen bbox {x1-x0}x{y1-y0}  ({100*(x1-x0)/W:.0f}% of width)")
    for lbl,p in [("TL",tl),("TR",tr),("BR",br),("BL",bl)]:
        print(f"   {lbl}  px=({p[0]:7.1f},{p[1]:7.1f})   norm=({p[0]/W:.4f},{p[1]/H:.4f})")
    lh = np.hypot(*(bl-tl)); rh = np.hypot(*(br-tr))
    print(f"   left edge height {lh:.0f}px, right edge {rh:.0f}px -> foreshortening {100*(1-min(lh,rh)/max(lh,rh)):.1f}%")
PY
[/external_agent_tool_call]

import React from "react";
import { SEC, TEACH } from "./tokens";

export type Chunk = { n: number; text: string; details: { n: number; text: string }[] };

/* The stimulus exactly as the drill renders it: every chunk in order, nothing
   marking one out from another. The teaching highlight is layered ON TOP and is
   the only thing here the real screen does not do. */
export const Stimulus: React.FC<{
  chunks: Chunk[];
  activeNs: number[];
  reading: boolean;
}> = ({ chunks, activeNs, reading }) => (
  <div
    style={{
      maxWidth: "52ch", color: SEC.ink, fontFamily: SEC.serif,
      fontSize: SEC.stimulusPx, fontWeight: 400, lineHeight: SEC.stimulusLh,
      fontOpticalSizing: "none", fontVariationSettings: '"opsz" 14',
    }}
  >
    {chunks.map((c) => {
      const on = activeNs.includes(c.n);
      return (
        <span
          key={c.n}
          style={{
            background: on ? TEACH.amberSoft : "transparent",
            boxShadow: on ? `inset 0 -2px 0 ${TEACH.amber}` : "none",
            borderRadius: 2,
            transition: "background 200ms",
          }}
        >
          {c.text}{" "}
        </span>
      );
    })}
  </div>
);

export const Captions: React.FC<{ text: string }> = ({ text }) =>
  text ? (
    <div style={{
      position: "absolute", bottom: 150, left: "50%", transform: "translateX(-50%)",
      maxWidth: 1180, padding: "14px 26px", borderRadius: 10,
      background: "rgba(8,6,20,0.9)", color: "#f8fafc",
      fontFamily: SEC.sans, fontSize: 30, lineHeight: 1.34, textAlign: "center",
    }}>
      {text}
    </div>
  ) : null;

/* Names the step she is in. The label a student reads for step 2 is always
   "Tell it back" (CONTEXT.md glossary), never "Say it back". */
export const StepRail: React.FC<{ step: string; note: string }> = ({ step, note }) => (
  <div style={{
    position: "absolute", left: 32, top: 28, zIndex: 5, width: 300,
    padding: "18px 22px", borderRadius: 12,
    border: `1.5px solid ${TEACH.amber}`, background: TEACH.panel,
    fontFamily: SEC.sans,
  }}>
    <div style={{ color: TEACH.amber, fontSize: 15, letterSpacing: 1.4, textTransform: "uppercase" }}>
      What he is doing
    </div>
    <div style={{ color: "#fff", fontSize: 32, marginTop: 8, fontWeight: 500 }}>{step}</div>
    <div style={{ color: TEACH.ink, fontSize: 19, marginTop: 10, lineHeight: 1.45 }}>{note}</div>
  </div>
);

import React from "react";
import { SEC, TEACH, LOGICAL_W, LOGICAL_H } from "./tokens";

/* Layout law for this video: the teaching layer gets its own 96px band across
   the top and NEVER overlaps the replicated app. The first cut floated the rail
   and the badge over the app's header rail and clipped it, which is exactly the
   confusion the Q1 decision forbids. */
export const BAND_H = 96;
const APP_H = 1080 - BAND_H;
export const APP_SCALE = APP_H / LOGICAL_H;          // 984 / 720
const APP_W = LOGICAL_W * APP_SCALE;
export const APP_X = (1920 - APP_W) / 2;

export const TeachBand: React.FC<{ step: string; note: string }> = ({ step, note }) => (

## assistant

There's a genuine wedge of original plate showing, widening toward the bottom. My corner detection used extreme-point heuristics, which are fragile on a rotated quad. Re-detecting by fitting lines to the actual edges instead.

## assistant

[external_agent_tool_call: Bash]
description: Re-detect quad by line fitting
command: cd /c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/video/public/plates
/c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/hyperframes-student-kit/.venv/Scripts/python.exe - <<'PY'
import numpy as np
from PIL import Image
from collections import deque

im = Image.open("room-v3.png").convert("RGB"); W,H = im.size
a = np.asarray(im).astype(np.int16)
mx = a.max(axis=2); mn = a.min(axis=2)
mask = (mn > 140) & ((mx-mn) < 34)

# isolate the monitor blob (largest component, downsampled flood)
small = mask[::8,::8]; lab=np.zeros(small.shape,np.int32); cur=0; best=(0,None)
for i in range(small.shape[0]):
    for j in range(small.shape[1]):
        if small[i,j] and lab[i,j]==0:
            cur+=1; q=deque([(i,j)]); lab[i,j]=cur; n=0
            while q:
                y,x=q.popleft(); n+=1
                for dy,dx in ((1,0),(-1,0),(0,1),(0,-1)):
                    yy,xx=y+dy,x+dx
                    if 0<=yy<small.shape[0] and 0<=xx<small.shape[1] and small[yy,xx] and lab[yy,xx]==0:
                        lab[yy,xx]=cur; q.append((yy,xx))
            if n>best[0]: best=(n,cur)
comp8 = (lab==best[1])
ys,xs = np.nonzero(comp8)
Y0,Y1,X0,X1 = ys.min()*8, ys.max()*8+8, xs.min()*8, xs.max()*8+8
sub = mask[Y0:Y1, X0:X1]
h,w = sub.shape
print("blob bbox", X0,Y0,X1,Y1, "->", w,"x",h)

# per-row extremes, ignoring the top/bottom 6% where corners round off
rows=[]
for r in range(int(h*0.06), int(h*0.94)):
    xs_=np.nonzero(sub[r])[0]
    if len(xs_)>w*0.35: rows.append((r, xs_.min(), xs_.max()))
R=np.array(rows,float)
def fit(col):
    A=np.stack([R[:,0], np.ones(len(R))],1)
    m,c=np.linalg.lstsq(A, R[:,col], rcond=None)[0]
    return m,c
ml,cl = fit(1); mr,cr = fit(2)

cols=[]
for c_ in range(int(w*0.06), int(w*0.94)):
    ys_=np.nonzero(sub[:,c_])[0]
    if len(ys_)>h*0.35: cols.append((c_, ys_.min(), ys_.max()))
C=np.array(cols,float)
def fitc(col):
    A=np.stack([C[:,0], np.ones(len(C))],1)
    m,c=np.linalg.lstsq(A, C[:,col], rcond=None)[0]
    return m,c
mt,ct = fitc(1); mb,cb = fitc(2)

def inter(m1,c1,m2,c2,vertical_first=True):
    # left/right lines: x = m*y + c ; top/bottom: y = m*x + c
    # solve x = m1*y + c1 and y = m2*x + c2
    y = (m2*c1 + c2)/(1 - m2*m1); x = m1*y + c1
    return x,y

TL = inter(ml,cl,mt,ct); TR = inter(mr,cr,mt,ct)
BR = inter(mr,cr,mb,cb); BL = inter(ml,cl,mb,cb)
print("\nfitted corners (normalised to full image):")
out=[]
for lbl,(x,y) in [("TL",TL),("TR",TR),("BR",BR),("BL",BL)]:
    gx,gy = (x+X0)/W, (y+Y0)/H
    out.append((gx,gy)); print(f"  {lbl}  [{gx:.4f}, {gy:.4f}],")
lh=np.hypot(out[3][0]-out[0][0], out[3][1]-out[0][1])
rh=np.hypot(out[2][0]-out[1][0], out[2][1]-out[1][1])
print(f"  left/right edge ratio {min(lh,rh)/max(lh,rh):.3f}")
PY
[/external_agent_tool_call]

## Q1 POV CUT BUILT AND RENDERED 2026-09-09
`projects/video/out/pov-q1.mp4` - 92.18s, 1920x1080, 30fps, h264+aac, 30.2 MB.
Composition `POVQ1` in `src/Root.tsx`. The old `RRCWalkthrough` is untouched.

### New files
- `src/pov/quad.ts` - homography -> CSS matrix3d, plus the DETECTED screen quad.
- `src/pov/beats.ts` - the loop as data (read / release / speak / confirm).
- `src/pov/Room.tsx` - plate, camera, monitor composite, optical defocus.
- `src/pov/Float.tsx` - Plate3D / SpeechBubble / CheckLine, kit techniques.
- `src/pov/POVWorking.tsx` - one stimulus in the room.
- `align/treat_audio.py` - the two-layer audio treatment.
- `recall/generate_recall.py` - cloned Recall generation.

### The room
`public/plates/room-v3.png`, 5504x3072, from `gemini-3-pro-image` at 16:9 / 4k.
Three variants generated; v3 chosen for the largest screen area (55% of frame
width), the study context, and the window placed right for the glance-off.

### Screen quad, and the two defects found by rendering
Detected by masking the light low-saturation region:
  TL 0.0712,0.1198  TR 0.6156,0.1839  BR 0.6052,0.7266  BL 0.0752,0.7500
- **Defect 1: composite undersized.** First render showed a grey wedge of the
  original plate down the right edge, widening toward the bottom. The mask stops
  inside the bezel because the screen vignettes at its border. A second detection
  by line-fitting DISAGREED with the first on the bottom edge, so both undershoot.
  Fixed with `OVERSCAN = 1.042`, pushing the quad out from its centroid so the
  composite buries its edge under the bezel. Verified by cropping the edge at
  high magnification: wedge gone.
- **Defect 2: plate edges exposed.** BASE_ZOOM 1.075 was not enough for a 232px
  glance; black bars appeared. The wrapper scales about its centre, so the safe
  translate is (zoom-1)/2*1920. Raised to 1.30. Costs nothing: the plate is
  5504px feeding a 1920px frame.
- Residual: a very thin light line still shows at some camera positions. Minor,
  not fixed, flagged to him rather than hidden.

### Audio treatment, verified
`align/treat_audio.py` splits on the real read spans from timeline.json.
Interior: highpass 95, lowpass 2350, +3.2dB at 220Hz, -4dB at 3kHz, volume 0.72.
Aloud: light early reflection only. Bed: 98Hz + 147Hz held sines, lowpassed to
420Hz, gated ONLY over interior spans with 0.45s/0.40s fades. No pulse.
Q1: 7 spans, 3 interior. Expected 92.12s, got 92.21s.
Render: mean -27.9dB, peak -6.0dB. Audio confirmed present and healthy.

### Cloned Recall audio, all three done
`recall/generated/PT105_S2_Q{1,2,3}.recall.wav` plus per-sentence timing JSON.
Q1 23.5s, Q2 29.4s, Q3 22.4s. Generated sentence by sentence so each piece stays
stable and the timing map falls out for free. NOT yet cut into the film.

### Verified this build
1. Duration 92.18s vs timeline 92.12s.  2. 1920x1080 h264 + aac.
3. Audio present, healthy levels.  4. Composite meets the bezel, no plate showing.
5. No frame edges exposed at full glance.  6. Correct chunk sharp in all three
   groups (checked group 0 at t=3 and group 2 at t=50).
EOF

## assistant

That's Q1. Ninety-two seconds, 1920x1080, your real audio, three full loops.

**What's in it.** He reads chunk one in his head while the camera tracks the line and the rest of the page sits out of focus. Focus releases, his eye glances off, the monitor slides to the edge of frame, and what he says arrives beside him on a glass card with real depth and its own bloom. Then his eye comes back, the page sharpens, and what he carried ticks off against it. Three times, on the real timings from his run.

**Two defects the render caught that no amount of planning would have.**

The composite was undersized. A grey wedge of the original plate showed down the right edge, widening toward the bottom, because the screen vignettes at its border so the detector stops short of the bezel. I re-detected by line-fitting, and the second method disagreed with the first about the bottom edge, which told me both were undershooting rather than one being wrong. Fixed by overscanning the quad so its edge buries under the bezel, then verified by cropping that edge at high magnification.

The camera pan exposed the plate edges as black bars. The glance is 232 pixels and the wrapper scales about its centre, so the zoom has to clear 1.25 for the translate to stay inside the image. It was at 1.075. Raised to 1.30, which costs nothing since the plate is 5504 pixels wide feeding a 1920 pixel frame.

**One thing I did not fix:** a very thin light line still shows at the screen's right edge at some camera positions. It's much smaller than it was and I'd rather you see it and tell me whether it bothers you than have me spend another render cycle on something you might not notice.

**Also done while that rendered:** all three Recall passages are generated in your cloned voice — 23.5, 29.4 and 22.4 seconds — with per-sentence timing maps. They aren't cut into the film yet, because the Recall beat needs its own picture design: Space pressed, page clears, and the whole argument given back to a blank screen.

Watch it and tell me what's wrong with it.

"

### How the fade is expressed (as data, not three hand-built compositions)
`Scaffold = 0 | 1 | 2` on `POVWorking`, driven from a `POV` array in Root.tsx.
- 2 (Q1): "He carried" label + up to 5 confirmations.
- 1 (Q2): confirmations only, label gone, 3 of them.
- 0 (Q3): no confirm card at all.

## assistant

Two framing bugs, same root cause. At zoom 1.30 the monitor is wider than the frame, so the app's left edge — including the footer instruction — is cropped. And the step label is pushed clean off the left of frame.

For an instructional video where the point is reading the screen, cropping the screen is the wrong trade. Reducing the zoom and the glance together.

## assistant

[external_agent_tool_call: Bash]
description: Reframe camera so the monitor is never cropped
command: cd /c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/projects/video
python - <<'PY'
import io
p="src/pov/Room.tsx"
s=io.open(p,encoding="utf-8").read()
s=s.replace("const GLANCE_X = 232;","const GLANCE_X = 96;")
s=s.replace("const GLANCE_Y = 46;","const GLANCE_Y = 26;")
s=s.replace("const BASE_ZOOM = 1.30;","const BASE_ZOOM = 1.12;")
s=s.replace('''/**
 * Push in far enough that panning never exposes the plate's edge.
 *
 * The wrapper is scaled about its centre, so the widest safe translate is
 * (zoom - 1) / 2 * frameWidth. The glance is 232px plus up to 9px of drift, so
 * zoom must clear 1 + 2 * 241 / 1920 = 1.251. The speak phase then pulls 0.012
 * back out. 1.30 leaves real headroom, and costs nothing: the plate is 5504px
 * wide feeding a 1920px frame.
 */''','''/**
 * Push in far enough that panning never exposes the plate's edge, and NO
 * further, because zoom crops the monitor.
 *
 * The monitor spans roughly x 136..1190 of an unzoomed frame. Scaling about the
 * centre by z puts its left edge at 960 + (136 - 960) * z, so z = 1.30 pushed it
 * to -111 and cropped the app's own footer instruction off the screen. In an
 * instructional film, cropping the thing being read is the wrong trade.
 *
 * At z = 1.12 the left edge sits at +37, and the widest safe translate is
 * (z - 1) / 2 * 1920 = 115, which comfortably clears a 96px glance plus 6px of
 * drift. The glance is smaller than it was and still unmistakable, because the
 * monitor visibly slides out of the reading position.
 */''')
s=s.replace("const drift = Math.sin(k * Math.PI * 1.6) * 9;","const drift = Math.sin(k * Math.PI * 1.6) * 6;")
io.open(p,"w",encoding="utf-8").write(s)

p="src/pov/Instruction.tsx"
s=io.open(p,encoding="utf-8").read()
s=s.replace('<Plate3D x={132} y={106} w={318}','<Plate3D x={170} y={112} w={318}')
io.open(p,"w",encoding="utf-8").write(s)
print("reframed")

## assistant

[external_agent_tool_call: Bash]
description: Checkpoint Round 5 build
command: cat >> /c/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/brainstorms/2026-09-09-rrc-instructional-video.md <<'EOF'

## ROUND 5 BUILD - the instructional cut (2026-09-10)
New compositions `InstrQ1` / `InstrQ2` / `InstrQ3`. The Round 4 POV cuts stay
registered as POVQ1-3 for comparison.

### One script drives picture AND audio
`align/build_script.py` emits BOTH `public/audio/<slug>.instr.mp3` and
`public/content/<slug>.script.json` from the same pass, so they cannot drift.
Beat kinds: card / read / release / speak / press / recall / settle.
Each spoken beat carries `srcStart`, mapping it back onto the ORIGINAL recording
so the caption cues still resolve after teaching beats are cut into the timeline.
That mattered: in Q1 the Colouring In card sits BETWEEN release and speak, so a
single per-group offset would have been wrong.

| cut | beats | was | now | teaching added |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | 16 (4 cards) | 92.12s | 138.42s | +46.3s |
| Q2 | 9 (0 cards) | 102.96s | 134.86s | +31.9s |
| Q3 | 9 (0 cards) | 80.10s | 104.00s | +23.9s |
Q1 audio is 0.15s longer than the script: mp3 encoder padding, landing in the
trailing `settle` silence. Picture is cut to the SCRIPT, not the mp3.

### Reading is now genuinely silent
His voice is muted across every read span and the span keeps its real duration.
The held-tone bed carries those stretches alone, so its level was raised
(0.075 / 0.030 from 0.055 / 0.022) with 0.55s/0.5s fades.

### THREE FRAMING BUGS, all the same root cause
Everything inside the camera wrapper is scaled by the zoom, so anchors are in
ROOM space, not frame space: `frameX = 960 + (roomX - 960) * zoom`.
1. Confirm card ran off the right edge after zoom went 1.075 -> 1.30. (Round 4.)
2. **The zoom itself was cropping the monitor.** At 1.30 the app's left edge sat
   at -111, cutting off its own footer instruction "Read it. Tell it back and
   colour it in as you go". In an instructional film, cropping the thing being
   read is the wrong trade.
3. Step label at x=132 mapped to frame -116 and was entirely off screen.
**Fix: BASE_ZOOM 1.30 -> 1.12, GLANCE_X 232 -> 96, GLANCE_Y 46 -> 26, drift 9 -> 6.**
Monitor now spans frame 37..1218, fully visible. Safe translate at 1.12 is 115,
clearing a 96px glance plus 6px drift. The glance is smaller and still reads,
because the monitor visibly slides out of the reading position.

### Two smaller fixes
- `Plate3D` gained a `flow` mode. Instruction cards are NOT objects in the room,
  they are the film stopping to talk, so they lay out in normal flow and let a
  flex parent centre them. Absolute positioning had pinned the first card to the
  top-left corner, half off frame.
- The step label washed out completely over the white paper card. It now carries
  a dark backing (rgba(9,11,26,0.82)) rather than relying on glass.

### The Space press, built
`Screen` gained a `pressed` prop, 0..1, that depresses the app's OWN Space chip
with an inset shadow and a 1.6px translate. No hands are needed and nothing is
invented: the chip is really in the drill's footer. The stimulus clears at
k > 0.55 of the press beat and the footer switches to "Say the whole argument
back from memory." / "next stimulus", which is the real recalling state.
EOF

# Session Handoff — RRC POV instructional video: built from scratch to three rendered cuts

## Where it started
Joshua asked how to make videos with the newly installed Remotion plugin, which turned into building the RRC instructional video that a parallel session had left blocked. Two blockers that session declared unresolvable were both resolved here: the "his recordings read aloud but step 1 is silent" contradiction, and "no TTS available". The film went through five rounds of requirements grilling and three build passes; the current cut is the instructional one, with steps named, reading genuinely silent, and The Recall demonstrated.

## Decisions locked + what shipped
- **Voice: Chatterbox (MIT, local, CPU) cloning `voice-ref.mp3`, which is Joshua's own voice.** Kokoro was set up first, then abandoned once he chose to clone rather than use presets. All three Recall passages generated: `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\projects\video\recall\generated\PT105_S2_Q{1,2,3}.recall.wav` (23.5s / 29.4s / 22.4s).
- **Only The Recall is synthesized.** His real recordings carry steps 1 to 3. Reverses the earlier "re-voice everything" decision, which had rested on an objection that died once the clone was him.
- **Reading is SILENT**, not filtered. Read spans muted, real duration preserved so reading pace stays visible. Held-tone bed carries those stretches.
- **Steps named on screen: Reading / Tell it back / Colouring In / The Recall.** Uses the app's own student-facing labels, against Joshua's stated "Rephrase", because the app footer in the same shot already says "Tell it back".
- **Each step gets a full teaching stop on first encounter, then a persistent label.** Q1 carries all four stops; Q2 and Q3 carry none.
- **Annotations float in the room, not on the screen.** Round 1's "must never read as app UI" is satisfied by geometry, not styling.
- **Kling generates the room only.** It cannot render the stimulus text; the screen is always Remotion composited onto the monitor quad.
- **No disclosure of anything** — supersedes the earlier decision requiring a label on generated audio.
